DM: You keep the guards still and your voice low. Bede goes off at a trot. Corra stays beside you at the parapet with her hand on her sword, and neither of you says a word. Below, the scraping comes twice more, slow and patient, and stops each time you shift your feet.

Hodd answers in a whisper. The water at the stair's foot is about knee-deep this time of year, since the river is low in autumn. It runs waist-deep in spring floods. The vault has a grated drain culvert opening to the river, too small for a man, and an old tale of a sealed passage called the Dead Road, running toward the burial hill outside the walls. He has never seen it. For sealing, there are spare bridge capstones stacked in the quay yard, and the grating itself is iron-banded oak, good for wedging.

You could hang lamps from above, and you do. Hodd fetches two long lamp-poles from the bridge, and you hang four lit lanterns over the parapet, so that gold light falls down the stair's throat and shines on the wet steps. The scraping does not come again for a long while. You take that as a good sign, though you do not trust it.

Bede returns at half past five, red-faced and sweating, with two more guards, a sledge, an iron bar, and fifty flasks of oil in two sacks. That cost 5 gp, so you have 10 left. The sun is lower now, the river is going copper, and the first long shadows lie across the quay. Dusk is about an hour off.

You have two 1st-level slots, no Channel Divinity, Protection from Evil and Good ready with its powder, and Shield of Faith. Five guards and Hodd stand ready, the capstones are in the yard, and below the arch the lamplight holds.

DM: I can't resolve that exactly as you wrote it, so I'm stopping to ask. Shield of Faith and Healing Word both take a bonus action, and you only get one bonus action each turn. The rule for spells cast as a bonus action also says you can't cast another spell that turn, except a cantrip with a casting time of one action. So you can't do both on the same turn.

Here are the options, with the ghoul at the grating bloodied at about half its strength and you at 7 of 17 HP:

1. Shield of Faith on yourself (AC 20 for up to 10 minutes, concentration) as your bonus action. Your action could then be Sacred Flame on the ghoul (DEX save DC 13, 1d8 radiant) or a step back and the Dodge action.

2. Healing Word on yourself as your bonus action. It heals 1d4 + 3 + 3, about 8 to 12, and then Sacred Flame as your action.

3. Cure Wounds on yourself as your action. It heals 1d8 + 3 + 3, about 7 to 14, with no bonus-action spell on top of it.

You can also step back one pace first, since movement is free. That keeps you out of the ghoul's reach, and Bede and Corra can keep spearing it from the sides. Which do you choose?

You tell Hodd what you said you would. Two guards on the grating all night, every lantern lit, and a runner to the hospice at the first scratch. Hodd nods and says he'll stand the first watch himself, since he's the one who didn't look for thirty years.

At the hospice, the lamps burn by every cot as you asked. Rudd sleeps with his splinted thigh propped high, and his colour is better. The tanners' lane stinks, and the morning is cold. The door is the one with the pale bone nailed above it. Before you can knock, a voice from inside bellows, "If you're bleeding, come in. If you're selling, go to the devil!"

Inside is a low, hot room with a table scarred by knives, a shelf of splints and rolled bandages, and a heavy woman of sixty with forearms like a smith's. She is up to her elbows in a bowl of flour and looks you over without stopping. "A priest. You've the look of Edda's. Well? Speak."

What does Brannoc say?

== ROUND 70 ==
PLAYER: "Good morning, Mother Aldith. I'm Brannoc Ashdown, Edda Thorne's foster-son, and I've come with two matters. A boy named Tobin has a crushed ankle at Brother Ansel's hospice, and a dyer in Greywater, Perrin Gale, has a hip that healed crooked after a fall from a rack."

I hold out the note. "This is also from me, for a dyer's daughter named Wren, twelve, who sweeps the chapel there and wants to learn the work. Maud Marrow vouches for her, and her parents have agreed to a month's trial. Will you read it, and will you see Tobin today?"

DM: Aldith wipes her hands on her apron, takes the note, and reads it standing, with her lips moving. Then she reads it again.

"Edda Thorne's boy." She grunts. "Taught me to pack a wound with moss when I was nineteen and a fool. Is she dead?" You tell her, and she nods slowly and says nothing for a breath. "She'd have hated a fuss. Good."

Persuasion (18): she asks what you want in plain words and you answer in plain words, and it counts. Maud's name helps most. "Maud Marrow's blankets are the only reason I've not frozen these ten winters. If she vouches for the girl, that's one thing, and if the girl can count breaths and keep her mouth shut, that's another. Send her down with Maud when the weather holds. A month. If she faints at the first bad break, she goes home. If she doesn't, we'll talk."

On Tobin, she's already pulling her bag off a hook. "Crushed? Then I'll see it before the swelling sets harder. I'll come now. Your priest can hold the lad's shoulders while I do it." She stops with her hand on the latch. "What are these marks Ansel's boy was babbling about, round the ankle?"

You tell her briefly. She says nothing, her jaw hard, and then shrugs her bag onto her shoulder.

On Perrin, she's flatter. "A hip healed crooked a month ago? I'd have to break it again, and he has to come to me. I'll not trek upvalley for a man too proud to ask. Tell him that, and tell him I said it kindly."

It's a quarter to seven on Day 6. Aldith is ready to leave for the hospice, and she is looking at you to see if you are coming.

Healing Word is ready, with two 1st-level slots left. DM: Hob takes a long time to answer, as a man does who is counting things he can't see. "I do not count," he says at last. "I hear. Feet, and the drag of a heavy thing along stone. One came back hurt, and went under the stone, and did not run again, but I heard it dragging a leg when the flagstone shifted. Since the Long Cut, that one has not come back." He looks toward the corner. "I cannot say where the road comes up. The way is long, and under the hill it forgets whose it is."

He lifts the lamp-house lantern gently from its hook, trims the wick with a gray thumb, and hangs it again. "Tell Maud the lamp was lit. And tell Pell I like his spoons."

You leave ten of your fifty flasks, no, five, on the shelf by the door for Pell's watch, and tell him to send word through Brother Ansel if the stones ever stir. He promises, then adds with a grin that he's never carved a better spoon.

Hit points: take the fixed 5 (plus your Constitution +2, so 7 more hit points, for a maximum of 24), or roll a d8 and add 2 (at least 1 in all cases).

You may now prepare six spells from the whole cleric list after your next long rest, up from five. You can change them then.

Preserve Life now restores 15 hit points, divided among those you choose.

Hodd told the guard that the water under the second arch has been running oddly cold for two nights, and a mist has hung there at dawn.

At the hospice, Ansel takes Joss's letter in both hands and reads it standing under the shrine lamp. He says nothing for a while. Then he opens the book of the faithful on the lectern and writes the six names in his own hand, one by one, with the date. Tobin's ankle is splinted and set straight, and he is awake and boasting. Rudd is sitting up with a crutch, and Imma has been sent home with a headache and strict orders.

It's a quarter past nine on Day 9. You have all your slots and Channel Divinity. The cold water under the arch troubles you, and the bridge is two streets away. Ansel offers you your old cot.

The culvert's outlet lies on the east bank, forty paces downstream of the second arch. It's a stone-lipped arch-mouth in the quay wall, a hand's height above the low water, closed with iron bars two fingers apart. There's a flat ledge on the quay about ten feet above it, with a low rail, where the watch tie up boats. From there you have a clear view straight down onto the grate and a clear line to throw. The mist lies here as well, in a thin cold skin on the water, and the lantern light turns it a sickly yellow.

You set your shield, and your companions set their spears. Nothing moves. The drag you heard comes again from the dark behind the bars, nearer now, slow and wet. Then it stops. In the silence a long gray hand slides out between the bars, fingers spread wide, and rests on the stone lip as if feeling for warmth. It is blistered black along the back, and there's a spear-wound in the wrist that has never closed.

You know that hand. Hob said it dragged a leg.

It's twenty past ten on Day 9, and the ghoul is below you in the dark. It hasn't seen you yet. You have four 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, Channel Divinity, and a flask of oil in your hand. Corra and Bede stand either side of you.

Before you leave, Captain Voss, who walked with you, gives you one more thing: Reedholm. It is a fen village two days south on the river road, reed-cutters and eel-trappers, with a marsh-fever and no healer since old Mother Vane died in spring. The carters speak of lights seen over a drowned barrow in the reeds, but carters will say anything.

It's a quarter past one on Day 10. You have three 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, Channel Divinity, and thirty-seven flasks of oil. You could leave now for Reedholm, or rest in Stonebridge tonight and set out fresh in the morning.

  [game] notebook: updated Reedholm
DM: Your first question: yes, probably. Lesser Restoration touches a creature and ends one disease or one of four conditions: blinded, deafened, paralyzed, or poisoned. A marsh-fever is a disease, and it would end it. But it ends one disease on one creature per casting, and you have only two 2nd-level slots a day. If the whole village is sick, you can't cast your way through it. You'd have to use your Medicine to find who is worst off and who simply needs rest, water and a clean bed. It wouldn't touch what the fever left behind, such as weakness or a ruined lung.

You spend the afternoon and evening on errands. At the market you buy fifty feet of hempen rope (1 gp), a hooded lantern (5 gp, burns six hours on a flask, and you can lower the hood to dim it), and five more candles. That's 6 gp, and you're left with 4. Tobin shows you his ankle and asks about the hill. Rudd is up on his crutch complaining about the porridge. You sit with Imma's father at the cart-yard for a while, and ask the carters what you meant to.

You learn this. The river road south forks at a wooden milestone, the Eel Fork. The east branch goes to Reedholm along a causeway, two days on foot, with a ferry-rope at the Black Ford, which a boy keeps. The lights over the drowned barrow are pale green-white and seen on moonless nights, and the reed-cutters call them the Wick-fires. A boy named Sedge brought the first fever cases to market last week and went home sick. Voss, when you ask her, has nothing more. Nobody on her watch has been south of the Eel Fork this month.

You sleep late, from nearly midnight, under the shrine lamp. The long rest ends at twenty to eight on Day 11. You wake with 24 of 24 hit points, four 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, and Channel Divinity ready. Your prepared list is unchanged. You have thirty-seven flasks, two lanterns, rope, ten candles, and 4 gp. The morning is clear, and Ansel stands at the green door with a heel of bread and a twist of dried willow-bark for fever.

DM: You sit beside her and take her wrist. "Marta, you're safe now." You steep a pinch of Ansel's willow-bark in warm water over the fire, and she sips it gratefully, grimacing at the bitterness. Then you lay your hand on her burning forehead, say the prayer of Lesser Restoration, and spend one of your 2nd-level slots. The warmth gathers and goes in. It takes a heartbeat, and then her shaking stops. The spots at her wrists begin to fade, and the heat goes out of her skin like a tide going out. She draws a long, easy breath, the first in days, and begins to cry quietly.

The cure ends the disease, but not the weakness it left behind. She is still thin and worn, and she needs food and sleep. You give her bread and honey and a cup of broth made from your ration, and she eats like a famished child, and then she sleeps.

She wakes a little before midnight, clear-eyed, and tells you what she can. As far as she knows, about fifteen in Reedholm are sick, out of perhaps sixty. Three are dead: old Hobb Sallow, a baby, and a reed-cutter named Tam Rush. The sick are those who cut reeds at the Hollow Isle, a low drowned barrow in the fen where they cut thatch two weeks ago after the autumn gale stripped the roofs. The Wick-fires began that week. Her son Dab is five, with a mild fever, and he is with her sister Wenna. The headwoman, Ode Rush, will not let anyone cross to the Isle again.

And a long rest brings the slots back, though you can't take another until tomorrow morning at the earliest. So triage matters. Cure the worst, tend the rest, and find out where the fever comes from, or you'll be curing the same people for a month.

Ode Rush, as Pim and Marta describe her, is about fifty-five, broad and weathered, with a reed-cutter's scarred hands and a flat voice. She is proud and practical, and frightened under it. Her husband Tam died of the fever, and her nephew Jory is lost on the Isle. She'll want to see what you are before she trusts you.

Pim wishes you luck, and keeps the rope. The track beyond the ford runs along a raised bank through the reeds, with open water to the left and a low grey sky. You and Marta walk steadily, and she leans on your arm and tells you who is who as you go. A little before noon the reeds open, and Reedholm comes into view: twenty low houses on stilts and mud-brick plinths, thatched with reed and patched with new green bundles, joined by plank walks over the shallows. A bell hangs at a post by the landing, silent. Smoke rises thin from only a few chimneys. Eel-traps hang drying on every rail. No children play on the planks.

A broad woman in a patched oilskin stands at the end of the first walk, arms folded, a pole-hook in her hand. Behind her two men hold reed-knives, not drawn. She looks at the mace, the shield, and Marta on your arm.

"Marta Quill," she says. Her voice is flat. "You were to bring a healer. You've brought a soldier."

It's ten past noon on Day 13. You have four 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, and Channel Divinity. Marta is steady beside you.

What does Brannoc say?

That means no Lesser Restoration if the dead mound's chill takes anyone, and no Spiritual Weapon. The three Wick-fires turn. They don't drift like gas. They slide, all three together, slowly and deliberately, until they hang over the gap's mouth and the stones. Their light falls on the reeds around you. The cold sinks into your teeth, and the grave-earth smell thickens. Jory's arm drops from his face. His eyes are open and fixed on you, wide and bloodshot, and his lips shape the word "back" without a sound.

You are about twenty paces from him, and the lights are between you and him, over the gap. They haven't touched you yet. It's twenty-five minutes past seven on Day 15. You have three 1st-level slots and Channel Divinity. The skiff is a long way behind you on the rope.

What does Brannoc do?

DM: I'll stop before I roll anything, because your plan has a snag and you should know it first.

The burned mud-flat lies on the near side of Reedholm, well away from the stilts. By pole it's about twenty minutes from here, which is a little under 200 rounds. The village fires are another few minutes beyond.

Wisps don't fear fire. Their stat block gives them resistance to fire, and they fly, so a wall of burning oil on the water wouldn't block them. The one on your tail would simply float over it. Burning oil does the wisp little harm and the skiff some risk, and it wouldn't feed their light. What would help is distance and numbers. A crowd of spears wouldn't help much either, since a wisp is hard to hit and resists ordinary blades and bolts. A mace is nonmagical. What could hurt them is Sacred Flame, since radiant isn't one of their resistances, though they dodge it well. Your Guiding Bolt would hit hard if it landed, since it's radiant too, but you've only one slot left.

You could still pour the oil and light it as a gesture. It would cost you your action this round and do next to nothing to the wisp. Or you could do something else with the action.

It's round 10, and it's still your turn. You have 12 of 24 hit points, one 1st-level slot, and no Channel Divinity. What does Brannoc do?

To your question: yes, you would get death saves, but not before the danger. At 0 hit points you fall unconscious and begin death saves, which you roll at the start of each of your own turns. A will-o'-wisp's Consume Life is a bonus action on its own turn. If you're at 0 and it is within 5 feet, it can force a DC 10 Constitution save. On a failure you die outright, and it heals itself. Your +2 Constitution makes that save hard to miss, but it isn't impossible. A hit on an unconscious creature within 5 feet is also a critical hit, which counts as two failed death saves.

So the danger is real. You have no slots and no Channel Divinity. Protection from Evil and Good has about eight minutes. Behind the reeds on the water ahead, a faint line of smoke rises where Reedholm's fires burn. It's round 16, and it's your turn.

Then they turn and drift back across the water toward the Isle, and the fen is only mist.

They lift you out of the boat. Ode doesn't speak. She kneels and closes your eyes herself, and puts her scarred hand on your forehead for a moment, the way she did with Sedge. Jory, grey and shaking in his blanket, says only, "He came for me." Marta cries without sound. Sedge, who has been out of bed for two days, brings the bowl of broth you never got to eat and sets it by your hand, then does not know what to do and stands there.

They bury you on the dry rise above the Long House, where the first light comes. Ode says the words, and they are plain ones. Reedholm says your name as one of its own. Nobody takes a coin for anything that day. By the third week the thatch is new, the wells are boiled, the last of the sick are on their feet, and Bram Hale and Dessa Pike carry the stones for your cairn. Wick and Gorm go back to the Isle with half the village in daylight, with salt and fire and the old rites for the dead. They find the gap, and what they find in it is theirs to tell. Reedholm sends word to Stonebridge.

The word reaches Brother Ansel in nine days. He sits a long time with the letter in the hospice garden, then writes your name in the book of the faithful where it belongs. He sends riders to Greywater and Hollow Reach. Edda's wooden amulet came back from Reedholm in Marta's hands, wrapped in your blanket. Ansel turns it over for a night. Then he walks to Mother Aldith's with it and puts it in the hands of the girl who holds the lamp steady.

Wren is sitting on her stool by a bed when he comes in. She does not cry at first. She holds the amulet and asks whether it hurts to wear. Ansel says it did not hurt Edda, and it did not hurt Brannoc. She puts it on over her apron. Aldith, who has an opinion on everything, says nothing at all. The next morning Wren is up before dawn, counting breaths at a sick child's cot, humming a tune she doesn't know the words to.

The news goes up the valley. Maud Marrow hears it in Greywater and sits at her loom without weaving. Then she walks up the road to the Wayfarers' Lamp with a new blue shawl and her brother's old flint. Pell and Dorrin Harrow keep the rota there, and Pell has a good arm again and Dorrin a warm back. They say your name when they light the lamp. Hob Marrow, in his quiet, tells them he heard it already. He says the light was lit, and that is all he asks of any man. The lamp does not go dark.

And in a green-hulled skiff on the fen, a boy of nineteen blows his bone whistle once at dawn. He has stopped calling anything a dare. Jory Rush now learns the healing herbs from Marta Quill.

That's the end of Brannoc's story. He went out as he promised he would, and left nobody to suffer alone. Thank you for playing him so well.

DM: Here is Tamsin Vale, built from the SRD druid and Circle of the Land rules as written. I'll keep her numbers in my replies, since the sheet tool can't hold her.

**Tamsin Vale, human druid 3 (Circle of the Land, swamp)**
- Abilities: STR 13, DEX 14, CON 15, INT 11, WIS 16, CHA 9. These are the standard array (15, 14, 13, 12, 10, 8) with human's +1 to each.
- Hit points: 24. That's 10 at 1st level (8 + CON 2) and 7 each at 2nd and 3rd, taking the fixed 5 + 2, as we did for Brannoc. She has 3d8 Hit Dice.
- AC: 15. Leather armor is 11, plus DEX 2, plus the wooden shield's 2. Druids won't wear metal, so wood fits. Speed 30.
- Proficiency +2. Saving throws: INT and WIS, so INT +0, WIS +5. Skills: Nature +2 and Survival +5, from the druid list, plus Perception +3 by my ruling for a ferrier. By another ruling she's proficient with water vehicles.
- Spells: save DC 13, spell attack +5. She has four 1st-level and two 2nd-level slots. Cantrips are Guidance, Shillelagh, and Druidcraft (the circle's bonus cantrip).
- Always prepared from the swamp circle, and free: Acid Arrow and Darkness.
- She can prepare WIS 3 + level 3 = 6 spells of your choice. You've named Cure Wounds, Entangle, Goodberry and Fog Cloud. You can choose two more. I'd suggest Healing Word (a ranged fix for a bleeding friend) and Thunderwave, or Speak with Animals, since it's a ritual. You can swap them after any long rest.
- Wild Shape: twice per short rest, beasts of CR 1/4 or less with no flying or swimming. Natural Recovery: once a day on a short rest she can regain slots totalling up to 2 levels.
- Weapons: ferry pole (quarterstaff), +3 to hit, 1d6+1 (1d8+1 two-handed). With Shillelagh she uses WIS: +5, 1d8+3 magical. Sling: +4, 1d4+2.
- Gear: leather armor, wooden shield, ferry pole, sling and bullets, ferry-rope, explorer's pack, druidic focus, herbalism kit, 10 gp.

Which two spells would you like, or shall I use my suggestions? When you've told me, I'll open the story. It's a damp grey dusk, years after Brannoc, and Tamsin is walking the old river road toward Reedholm, with a green-white light in a dream she can't shake.

DM: It has rained all afternoon, and now the rain has thinned to a cold drizzle that beads on your leather and your pole. The old river road is more track than road here, a raised bank of packed clay and cut reed running through flat, grey fenland. Water lies on both sides, black and still. The reeds hiss in the wind, and the light is going the colour of old pewter.

You have walked three weeks from the far south, and you've said perhaps forty words to anyone since the last market. The dream that brought you hasn't changed. In it, pale green-white lights hang low over water, and under them is a drowned stone you have never seen. You woke sweating from it nine nights running, and on the tenth you took your pole and walked north.

Ahead, a channel of slow black water cuts across the road, perhaps forty paces wide. A ferry-rope is strung from post to post. You notice, as a ferrier would, that the rope is well kept, newly tarred, and the posts are sunk deep. A flat punt, broad and clean, is moored on the near bank beside a low reed-and-timber hut with smoke rising from its chimney. A big grey dog lies on the doorstep with its head on its paws, and it lifts its eyes to you but not its body.

A man of about twenty-five sits on the punt's edge, mending a net by the last of the light. He is lean and sun-browned, and he has seen you well before you were close. He sets the net down slowly and rises with one hand resting on the rope. "Road's wet," he says. "Ferry's a copper a head after dark, two if you want the lantern."

Behind him on the far bank, small and low in the dusk, a few lights are lit among the reeds, yellow ones, the warm kind that come from windows. They are not green or white.

You have a full complement of slots and 24 of 24 hit points. The pole is in your hand, and the dog is watching you.

What does Tamsin do?

DM: You stop at the edge of the bank and look at the rope before you look at him. You run your eye along the tar, the knots and the depth of the posts. "Good rope. Whoever tars it knows the work."

Something in his shoulders loosens. He takes the coppers and nods once, and the dog on the step lets its head drop. "I do," he says. "Learned it from a man who learned it before I was born." He looks at the pole in your hand, then at the way you stand with your weight low, and almost smiles. "Southern ferrier, you said. You'd know how to read this water, then. It looks slack and it isn't. There's a pull under the middle at ebb."

You take the stern pole and he takes the rope. The channel is as he says, quiet on top and strong below, and you feel it in your wrists at the midpoint, a slow steady drag toward the west. He watches you correct for it without being told, and he says nothing, which from a man like him is respect. The lantern hangs from the bow-post, yellow and small, throwing a ring of light onto the black water.

Midway, you ask your questions. The village across the water is Reedholm, he says. "Reed-cutters, eel-trappers, and a landing that gets busier every year." Then you say the words "drowned stone" and "lights over the water," and his hand stops on the rope for one breath. Insight (15): it isn't surprise. It's the stillness of a man who has been told a thing like this before and hoped never to be again. He doesn't look at the west.

"There's a healer in the village," he says carefully. "Wren. She came up from the south as a girl and she's been here longer than I've had this rope. She wears an old wooden amulet, plain as a stick. If anyone knows about stones and lights, it's her. But she's not an easy woman. She'll test you before she talks. Don't flatter her."

The far bank rises out of the dusk, with plank walks over the shallows and low houses on stilts, new thatch bright against the old. You can smell smoke, eel and tar. The punt kisses the bank. Pim hands the rope to the dog, which lies down on it as though it were its job. He says, "The Long House is the big hall by the landing. She'll be there or near it." He looks at you a moment longer, then adds, "Mind the west. We don't go on the water out there after dark."

It's a quarter to six on a damp evening. You are at the edge of Reedholm with a lantern, your pole, a full set of slots, and 24 of 24 hit points. The few yellow lights ahead are lit.

The woman who stands there is in her late twenties, lean, in a plain apron over a worn wool dress, with dye-stained fingers and a face that gives nothing away. Around her neck, on a cord, hangs a small plain amulet of dark wood, rubbed smooth. She looks at the pole, at your boots, and at your hands. She does not look at your face for some time.

You say your name and what you've been dreaming. Her expression doesn't change. "Pim sent you," she says, flat. "He always does. He thinks I'm a priest." She turns without waiting. "Come in or don't, but shut the door. The damp gets in."

Inside, the hall is long and warm, and half its pallets are empty. A few are not. A woman nurses a baby by the stove, and an old man sleeps with his mouth open. At the far end a small girl of six or seven lies curled on a pallet, flushed and breathing fast. Wren walks straight to her. Without looking back, she hands you a lantern and a basin.

"I don't talk to people who've just walked in out of the rain with a dream," she says. "I talk to people who can hold a light steady. Sit there. Tell me what you see. Don't tell me what you think I want to hear."

The child on the pallet is pale and sweating, with a dry cough. Her lips are cracked, and one hand twitches against the blanket. Wren stands with her arms folded, watching you instead of the girl.

It's six o'clock on a damp evening. You have a lantern, a basin, a pole, and a full set of slots. What does Tamsin do?

I didn't know they'd gone back to it until Nell took sick." She looks at you flatly. "All four. The same evening. I didn't want to say it to anyone. She says Fen started it last month, after Jory Rush told the children the tale of the bone whistle at a harvest fire. Jory is a grown man now and has kept the fire-tale for years. He told it for the old reason, so they'd stay off the water, not to start a game.

You hood your lantern to a hand's width of light and stand at the end with your pole across your body, looking west and saying nothing.

Perception (22): the Hollow Isle is a long low shape a mile off, and over its west side three pale green-white lights hang very still. That is the dream, exactly, and it chills you. It is smaller and dimmer, and it holds there, neither coming nor going, as if waiting for a candle to be raised. On the platform at your feet lie five stubs of wax, a child's offering. The boards around the post are wet in a pattern that doesn't match the dew. A trail of small bare footprints, slick with water, runs from the edge of the platform up the walk toward the village. They are very small. They stop at the Long House door.

You have two 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, and 24 of 24 hit points. The lantern is hooded and the pole is in your hands.

Then she takes you by the sleeve, which she has not done before, and leads you along the front of the hall to the corner post by the landing stair. Carved into the grey wood in rows, in different hands and different years, are names. Under each is a small mark, a reed or a wave. They are the drowned. Most are old, worn smooth by hands. The last row is newer. Wren holds her lamp to it and puts her finger under the final name.

MINA TARR.

"Pim's," she says. "Not his blood. A fever-orphan he took in when he was sixteen and had nothing. She was six. Nine years ago, in the spring flood, the punt rolled at the Black Ford. He got out. She didn't. They never found her." She doesn't blink. "There's no grave. Pim won't say her name. He still tars that rope every month, and I think it's for her."

You take out the tallow stub you pocketed from the west walk, the one with the wick tied in a small neat knot. You show it to her. Wren looks at it a long time. Then her face does something it hasn't done all night. "She tied her wicks like that," she says. "She said it kept the light in. Half the village's children copied her, after. I thought it was just a game."

So the thing under the floor has a name, and the ford where it was lost is a mile back along the causeway. It is twenty-five past eight on a damp night. As the weight leaves the water, the pale sparks among the niches slow, hang, and turn all together like a shoal of fish. They drift after you, slowly and without hurry, a few paces behind. They don't rush you and they don't touch you. They only follow, the way the fourth light once followed a candle. The cold at your back is not the sarcophagus. It is the small bundle between your shoulders, and it feels, oddly, like a child holding on.

They carried salt and fire and the old rites, and they came back grey in the face. They found the same tomb and the same three marks, and they left the lid alone. "We said the words over it and sealed the crack with clay, and we came home," she says. "We told ourselves it was enough."

Wren is turning the dark amulet at her neck. She says, to you and not to Ode, "That's the same lamp. The one carved on the stone. There's a Wayfarers' Lamp at a village called Hollow Reach, a long way up the river, and a keeper there who was dead and still kept it lit. A man told me about it once. He told me a lot of things." She pauses. "He died out on that fen, three weeks after he cured half this village. It was the same lights, and the same Isle." She doesn't look at you. "He's the reason I have my trade. I'd thank you not to make anything of that."

It's half past ten on Day 2. You have 24 of 24 hit points, four 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, and Wild Shape ready. I give you 150 XP by my ruling for bringing Mina home, 1,250 in all. That's all I remember. He said it like it mattered."

By a quarter past eleven you are in Gammer Hild's house, a stilted hut with a window full of drying herbs, and you've brought her a spoonful of honey. She is tiny, bright-eyed and bent like a hook, and she eats the honey with great ceremony before she speaks. Her tale, in her cracked voice, goes like this.

In the old days, she says, there were three keepers for the road the dead walk. One kept the lamp, one cut the reed, and one watched the wave. The Lamp-Keeper sat at the road's head, the Reed-Keeper at its heart, and the Wave-Keeper at its tail, where the road runs into water. "That's the Isle, girl. The seventh barrow, where the road meets the fen." The tomb under it, she says, belongs to the Wave-Keeper's wife, who was set to watch the water-road when the keepers made their seals. "They say she was buried standing, with her face to the west, and a stone on her breast so she'd never rise and walk." She wags a finger. "The frost isn't hers. The frost is what she's holding down."

She cannot say more. The tale breaks off in places, as old tales do. She doesn't know what the frost is, only that it came from the road, long ago. "The Lamp is the first seal," she says, "and the Wave is the last. If you break one, the others strain. A keeper told me that when I was a girl. I thought she meant a boat."

It's a quarter past eleven on Day 2. You have 24 of 24 hit points, four 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, and Wild Shape ready. Gammer Hild watches you with her bright eyes, spoon in hand. Out past the shutters the three lights are pale over the water, and Wren stands in the doorway, listening with her arms folded.

What does Tamsin do?

Investigation (17): the margins are full of a neat, patient hand. Most of it is small things, such as doses and a reminder to hum slowly when a child is afraid. Some of it matters. At the Wayfarers' Lamp, Hob told him the Lamp "holds the road at its head" and must be tended by living hands as well as the dead keeper. At the Long Cut and the Drowned Stair he noted that each barrow of the Chain is held by a light, a reed-bond or running water, and that where one fails, the dead walk to the next. Tucked between the pages are a pressed willow leaf, a twist of blue thread, and a careful drawing of the three marks: lamp, reed and wave.

On the last written page, in a different, rushed hand, written in Reedholm, are four lines. "Seventh mound is the foot of the road. Not a tomb of one. A door. Do not break the lid. The lights are the lock, not the guard."

Wren reads it over your shoulder and her mouth goes thin. "He never told me that," she says. "He told me to boil the water."

It's noon on Day 2. You have 24 of 24 hit points, four 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, and Wild Shape ready. DM: You wait a few minutes for the first grey light rather than work blind beside that crack. "Pim, hold the skiff quiet. We start the moment I can see the clay in my hands." Then you press the plug in with your bare palm, from the shore side, never touching the lid. That is when the lights wake. I rolled for it, and the die came up a natural 1. Across the mound, to the west, the three pale lights that have hung still for days lift in the grey like three heads turning on a pillow. Tar is on his feet at the water's edge with his teeth bared and not a sound out of him. Pim has his pole in both hands and his face has gone the colour of tallow. The rushlight is lit in your hand, and the plug and the ring are done. The words are still unsaid.

DM: You breathe out and step back from the ring. "Pim, that's the first door shut. Stay in the skiff with Tar." He nods without a word. Does Pim hear anything from the crack? I rolled for it, and the roll was high, which means nothing. He sits with his head tilted and listens for a long time, and tells you afterward that all he heard was a quiet, settling sound, like a house after the fire has died. Tar's hackles lie flat again.

As for the rushlight, a twist of rush-pith and tallow burns an hour or so, no more. It will burn out well before noon. Hild's rite wants a new one lit each dusk, and that is the work you set yourself. You give the box of five to Pim, and he holds it against his chest as he would hold a loaf. "I'll come at dusk," he says. "Every one. I'll bring Tar. I'm the one who ought to." You don't argue.

You do not go near the west gap. You keep the mound between you and the lights, and Pim poles quietly through the reeds and out into the open water. Behind you the Isle sinks into the morning mist. The three lights stay over the west flank, pale and watchful, and then they are only a smudge.

Reedholm's landing comes up out of the grey at five past seven. Ode stands at the end of the walk with the horn still in her hand and her face unreadable, and Wick and Gorm are beside her. Wren is farther back, arms folded, counting heads in the skiff, and her shoulders drop when she gets to three.

You have 24 of 24 hit points, four 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, and Wild Shape ready. The north crack is sealed, the first rushlight has burned out by now, and the west gap and the frost-rimed sarcophagus are exactly as they were.

  [game] notebook: updated Hollow Isle
DM: You walk to the square and look the silent bell over. It hangs from an oak post that is older than any house nearby. It is bronze, as tall as your knee, and green all over with a crust of verdigris. The lip is thick and dull. The clapper is gone, and there is no ring or hook on the inside where one might have hung. Ode, watching from the Long House step, says the bell was never rung in her lifetime or her mother's, and that nobody remembers who hung it.

Investigation (11): you find the three marks cut round the shoulder, the lamp, the reed and the wave, much worn. You can see that a line of old script runs along the lip, but the verdigris has eaten it. You can't read a word. Sand and vinegar would clean it, and it would take an hour or so.

You ask Gammer Hild your questions, sitting beside her stool in the sun. Yes, she says, it must be rung from a boat, off the west gap, with the gap in front of you. As for a clapper, she waves one hand. "A bell with no tongue is struck with a hand and a word," she says. "A ferrier knows the word." You ask her what the word is. She frowns, sucks her gums, and shakes her head slowly. "If I knew it, girl, I'd have said it years ago." You can't tell whether that is the truth or only the truth as far as she remembers.

You ask Ode whether the bell can be taken down and carried. She considers it with her arms folded. "It can," she says. "It'll take four men and the winch from the eel-weir. I'll not have it done until you tell me it's needed. And I'll not have it done after dark." You say you won't do any of it until you understand the whole of the rite.

It is nearly two o'clock on Day 4. You have 24 of 24 hit points, four 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, and Wild Shape ready. The bell hangs in the square in the sun, silent. Pim will cross at dusk to light the rushlight, and the west gap and the frost sarcophagus are as they were.

DM: You fetch sand and vinegar from Wren's stores and set to work on the lip of the bell, slowly, with a soft cloth, so that you don't scratch the old script. Tobb holds the bucket and doesn't talk, which is a mercy. The verdigris comes away in green flakes and then in a dull paste, and under it the bronze is the colour of dark honey.

Investigation (12), just enough. The letters are old river-Common, cut by a patient hand, and some are worn to ghosts. After an hour and some squinting you have it, running once round the lip:

CALL THE FARE. PAY THE FARE. HERE THE ROAD ENDS.

You sit back on your heels. You know those words, or the first of them. In the south, where you worked, the ferrier's cry at a landing is "Fare!" It is what you shout across the water to say you're ready to carry. Every ferrier calls it. And when a passenger dies on the crossing, or a body is brought to the water, a coin is laid on the tongue to pay the fare, so the ferrier will carry the dead across and not leave them standing on the bank. Your mother taught you that before she taught you to read a current. It is a kind of promise, not a charm.

Wren, called to look, stands at your shoulder with her arms folded and reads it twice. She says nothing for a long time. Then, flat: "Pay the fare. Brannoc never mentioned that." She frowns at the bell. "If it's a debt, you'd better know who owes it, and how much, before you ring."

You write the lines in your notes. Gammer Hild, shown the script, sits very still and then says, in a small voice, "Aye. That's the word." She doesn't say what it means to her. You can see she is troubled.

It's ten past three on Day 4. You have 24 of 24 hit points, four 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, and Wild Shape ready. The bell hangs clean and gleaming in the square. It is not yet dusk, and Pim waits at the landing for the hour to cross.

What does Tamsin do?

DM: "Then the fare is the question," you say. "Gammer, who owes it? The dead on the Isle, the Wave-Keeper's wife, or the village for not paying all these years?"

Insight (13), just enough. Hild answers slowly, and you believe her. The village owes it, she says. The first families who came to the fen swore to pay a fare for the held dead once a year, and they did, until the Burning. After that no one remembered the oath, and the village went on living on the debt. "The fare is not a coin, girl," she says. "A coin's the sign of it." Then she shuts her mouth and won't be moved.

You ask for your own side of it. In the southern trade, the family pays the fare for their dead, a coin laid on the tongue before the body goes into the boat. If there is no family, the ferrier pays out of their own purse, so the dead never stand on the bank unpaid. That has always been the law of the landing, and you have paid it twice yourself, for a stranger drowned off a barge and for a child no one claimed.

Wren fetches a copper from the Long House box and places it in your palm without a word. You tuck it into your pouch. When the afternoon is gone, you go to the landing with Pim and Tar and cross at dusk with a rushlight and a flint. You take the north shore, as before, with the mound between you and the lights.

You land at a quarter to seven. The plug is where you left it, dry and pale, with the first rushlight burnt to a black stub at its foot. The salt ring has not been scuffed. You strike a flame to the new rushlight and set it against the clay. Then, as you say the words again, "Hold, door; keep, light; the road ends here," something knocks from inside the plug.

It is three knocks, slow and soft, like knuckles on the far side of a door. I rolled for it, and that was the low result. Pim goes grey. Tar whines once and presses against your leg. The rushlight doesn't waver. In the west, the three pale lights lift and hum and drift in a slow uneasy circle round the gap, but they do not come. After a long minute the knocking stops, and the little flame stands straight.

You have 24 of 24 hit points, four 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, and Wild Shape ready. A copper is in your pouch. It is nearly seven on Day 4 and the light is going. The second rushlight is lit, and the lights are restless in the west.
